I've always been the kind of guy who just takes things as they come. If it weren't for that blasted tablet and the new software they sprang on us down at the warehouse, I'd be perfectly content. It's just that one single thing that's got my stomach in knots. Everything else in my life is just... well, just normal, everyday life, and I'm perfectly fine with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worry about the new inventory system and your job performance feels uncontrollable or that something bad will happen if you make a mistake with it?\n\nPatient: Uncontrollable? No, no, I wouldn't use a word like that. It’s not like it takes over my brain when I don't want it to, like I was saying before about the weekends and my time with Mary. I can control my thoughts just fine when I'm away from the place, sitting in my recliner or out at the market. \n\nAnd as for something bad happening if I make a mistake... well, let me explain how it works down there on the floor. See, if you mess up on the tablet—and I have, I'll be the first to admit it, my fingers just don't glide over that glass screen the way the young kids' do. Just last week, Thursday I think it was, I was trying to log a shipment of those big yellow car-wash sponges we get in from a supplier over in Ohio. Good quality sponges, we've stocked them for years, usually go right to Aisle 7. Anyway, I was supposed to hit \"Received\" on the screen, and I think my knuckle bumped \"Pending\" or something like that. The screen flashed a different color, and I just stood there looking at it, not knowing how to go back. \n\nNow, did I think the warehouse was going to explode? No. Did I think the police were going to show up or I'd be ruined? Course not. The worst that happens is young Greg—he's the floor manager now, nice enough kid, maybe 26, drives a little red Honda Civic that he parks near the dumpsters—Greg just has to come over, sigh a little bit, tap a few buttons, and fix it. He doesn't yell, he just says, \"Let's fix that, Bob, watch how I do it.\" \n\nSo I know I'm not going to get hauled off to jail or fired on the spot, and I know it's not the end of the world. But it's just... it's the embarrassment of it, doc. It's feeling like a dinosaur in a place I used to know like the back of my hand. I'm not terrified of a disaster, I'm just dreading the sheer frustration of knowing I'm going to struggle with the blasted thing every single shift. I hate feeling like I can't do my job the way I've done it perfectly well for two decades. So no, I wouldn't say the worry is out of my control, and I don't think anything terrible is going to happen. It's just a miserable, heavy feeling of not being able to do what I'm supposed to do, and I just hate having to face it.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress about work affected any other parts of your life, like your relationships, your ability to enjoy activities, or your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I hate to admit it, but yes, it actually has started bleeding over into things. It’s embarrassing, really. You know I mentioned young Greg earlier, the floor manager? Well, it’s gotten to the point where he actually had to pull me into that little glass office they have right next to the loading bays. It was a Tuesday, I remember, because the big freight trucks from Chicago always come in on Tuesdays and it’s loud as all get-out in that part of the building. Anyway, I sat down, and he had this manila folder on his desk, and he told me he had to officially document some problems with my reliability. \n\nMe! I haven’t had a reliability issue in twenty years! I've always been the guy who shows up early. But because I’m struggling so much with that tablet, getting so far behind, and having to step away when my chest gets tight just to catch my breath, my numbers are way down. He was real gentle about it, I'll give him that, but he actually suggested I look into taking a medical leave. Medical leave! I’ve never taken a leave in my life, I'm not a fragile guy, but he said I just don't seem like myself and maybe I need time to get things sorted out. \n\nAnd I suppose he’s right, because it is affecting my life outside the warehouse, too. Mary and I, we go to Grace Methodist over on 4th Street. We've been members there since David and Sarah were in Sunday school, and I’ve been the head usher for the 10 AM service for, oh, going on fifteen years now. It’s a real responsibility, you know? You have to coordinate the bulletins, make sure old Mrs. Gable gets her aisle seat because of her walker, organize the collection plates, make sure the microphones are turned on for the choir... people really count on me to make sure the morning runs smooth. \n\nBut lately... I just can't do it. I had to call Pastor Jim just last week and tell him I need to pull back from all my church duties for a while. I felt terrible about it, just awful, leaving them in the lurch like that, especially since they rely on me to lock up the fellowship hall after coffee hour. But by the time Sunday morning rolls around, I’ve already got this heavy dread sitting on me about Monday, and I just can't muster the energy to put on my suit, stand at those double doors, and smile at everybody. I just don't have the spirit for it right now. So, yes, doc, I guess it really is taking a toll on things I used to take a lot of pride in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite related to how you’ve been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep and appetite... well, let me think on that. No, no, I really can't say I've noticed any changes there. \n\nLet me tell you about my routine. Mary and I, we've always been creatures of habit, you see. Every night, after we finish up the dinner dishes—I wash, she dries, that’s just how we’ve always done it—we sit down in the living room. I've got my brown La-Z-Boy, she's got her floral armchair, and we turn on Channel 4 for the local news at 10 o'clock. We like the anchor there, Tom, he’s a straight shooter. Anyway, once the weather report is done, usually right around 10:25, we turn off the set and head up to bed. I'm usually out like a light by 10:45. \n\nNow, sure, at 74 years old, I might have to get up once in the middle of the night to use the restroom, but that's just par for the course, right? I go right back to bed, pull up the covers, and I'm back to snoring. We bought one of those firm mattresses over at the mattress place by the mall about five years ago, and it does wonders for my back. I sleep straight through until my alarm goes off at 6 AM, just like I have for decades. So, no tossing and turning, no lying awake staring at the ceiling. \n\nAnd my appetite, well, that's just fine, too. Mary is a fantastic cook, always has been. Just last night she made her pot roast with the little red potatoes and carrots—the way she slow-cooks it, the meat just falls right apart on your fork. I had two helpings! And this morning, I had my usual: two eggs over easy, two slices of whole-wheat toast with just a little scrape of butter, and a hot cup of black coffee. I've always been a meat-and-potatoes kind of guy, and I still look forward to every meal. \n\nYou’d think with my stomach in knots over that blasted tablet system during my shift, I wouldn't want to eat a thing, but once I'm home and I smell what Mary's got on the stove, I eat just as much as I ever did. So, no, doc, eating and sleeping are about the only things that still feel completely normal these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you aren’t getting as much pleasure out of things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Pleasure out of things... well, let me think on that for a second. No, I wouldn't say that's happening. No. \n\nBecause, see, when I'm not at that warehouse, or when I'm not sitting there dreading going *to* the warehouse, I'm still me. I still like what I like. Take my garden, for example. Now, Mary and I have lived in our house on Maple Drive since 1978. Bought it for a song back then, if you can believe it, a nice little three-bedroom ranch. And out back, right past the patio, I've got this vegetable garden. I've been tending that exact same patch of dirt for over forty years. \n\nJust this past Saturday, I was out there. The sun was shining, it was maybe about 70 degrees, just perfect weather, really, not too humid yet. I was out there tying up the tomato plants to the stakes—I always grow the Big Boy tomatoes, plus a few rows of those little cherry tomatoes for the grandkids, because they just love to pluck them right off the vine and pop them in their mouths when they come over. And I tell you, doc, getting my hands down in that soil, smelling the damp earth after Mary watered it the night before... it feels just as good as it always has. I still get a real kick out of seeing those first little green tomatoes start to pop up on the vines. I can spend three, four hours out there and lose all track of time, just weeding and pruning, and I enjoy every single minute of it. \n\nAnd then in the afternoons, I'll go into the garage. I've got this old 1968 Ford pickup I've been tinkering with since I bought it off my brother-in-law back in '95. Nothing major, just keeping her running, polishing the chrome, cleaning the carburetor. I can pull up my stool out there, turn on the radio—usually I listen to the oldies station, they play all the good stuff from the sixties and seventies, none of this new noise—and I'm as happy as a clam. \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost my enjoyment of things. When I'm actually able to put work out of my mind—which, granted, is getting harder and harder to do once Sunday afternoon rolls around—but when I'm just living my life, doing my hobbies, I still enjoy them just as much as I did ten, twenty years ago. The joy is still there. It's just that blasted inventory tablet hanging over my head the rest of the time that makes me miserable. But my garden and my truck? No, those are still my pride and joy.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that even when you're doing the things you enjoy, like gardening or working on your truck, they don't feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doc, like I was just saying, it’s really not like that at all. Maybe I didn't explain it right, but when I'm out there doing my things, the satisfaction is a hundred percent still there. \n\nTake the truck, for instance. Just this past Sunday afternoon—this was right before the Sunday evening dread really started creeping in on me, you know—I was out in the garage with a microfiber rag and a tin of that Turtle Wax. You know the kind in the little green tub? Smells a bit strong, like chemicals, but I tell you, it gets a shine on old chrome like nothing else on the market. I spent a good hour just buffing the front bumper of that '68 Ford. Just rubbing it in little circles, focusing on the metal, listening to the oldies station. \n\nAnd when I finally stepped back and wiped away the haze, and I could see my own reflection clear as day in that bumper... well, I felt real proud. Real satisfied, you know? It felt just as good as it did the day I bought it from my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary took decent care of it, sure, but I’ve always kept it pristine. \n\nSo, no, the pleasure isn't gone one bit. The activities themselves feel exactly the same, they're my refuge, really. It’s just getting my mind off the warehouse and that blasted tablet long enough to go out and do them that's the trick. But once I'm actually doing them? I still love every single second of it. If I lost the joy in my truck or my tomatoes, well, I don't know what I'd do, but thank the good Lord that hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about work has started to generalize to other areas of your life, even those that used to feel safe or predictable?\n\nPatient: Generalize? You mean, am I getting all worked up and nervous about other things, like my regular day-to-day stuff? Well, let me think on that for a second... no. No, I really don't think so. It’s strictly the warehouse. \n\nBecause, you see, everything else in my life makes sense. Everything else is just the way it’s always been. Take Sunday mornings, for example. You’d think with me being so tied up in knots over that new computer tablet at work, I might be getting nervous about other kinds of paperwork or numbers, right? But no, not at all. Every Sunday morning, right after Mary and I finish our coffee—we still use this old stainless steel percolator we got for our twenty-fifth wedding anniversary, makes a much better, hotter cup than those new plastic drip machines they sell nowadays—we sit down at the kitchen table. I get out the checkbook, the one with the blue leather cover, and I go through the mail. \n\nI open up the bills—the electric, the water, the property tax when it comes around in the fall. I sit there with my favorite ballpoint pen, the metal one I got from the bank years ago, and I write out every single check by hand. I balance the ledger down to the very last penny. I’ve done it exactly that way since, oh, probably 1974. And I don't feel an ounce of worry when I do it. I don't get sweaty palms or a racing heart. It's safe, it's predictable, and most importantly, it makes sense to me. I'm in control of it. \n\nSame goes for just running errands. I drive down to the Ace Hardware on Main Street to pick up fertilizer or a new spark plug, and I know exactly where everything is in that store. I know the owner, Bill, we talk about the weather for ten minutes, I pay with cash, and I drive home. It’s just easy. Or mowing the lawn! I've got this Toro push-mower, gas-powered. You prime it three times, pull the cord, and it starts. Every time. There are no passwords to remember, no error screens flashing at you, no supervisor breathing down your neck telling you you scanned the wrong barcode on a pallet of dog food. \n\nSo, no, doc, the rest of my life is perfectly fine. The anxiety hasn't bled over into my home or my marriage or my chores. It's just that one specific place, that warehouse, and that one specific machine that's ruining my peace of mind. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me, going about my business exactly the same way I always have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now that you put it that way... I suppose, yes, there have been some things slipping. It's hard to admit, because like I said, I like to keep things orderly and I pride myself on being a dependable guy. But take my church, for instance. Mary and I have been going to First Methodist down on 4th Street for over forty years. And for the last fifteen of those years, I’ve been the head usher for the 9 AM service. I get there early, I hand out the bulletins, I help the older folks find their seats, and I always organize the men's group to set up the folding tables for the monthly pancake breakfast. People really count on me to get all that done, you know? 

But these last few weeks... I just haven't had it in me. I actually called Pastor Miller last week and told him I had to step back from my church duties. I just couldn't face the crowd, couldn't put on a happy face and shake hands when my stomach is tied in absolute knots. Mary went to service by herself this past Sunday, and I just sat in my armchair in the living room feeling terrible about it. I've pulled back from the very people who rely on me, and I hate letting them down.

And honestly, it’s getting to the point where folks are noticing at the job, too, which is just the most shameful part of it all. I know you asked about things outside of work, but I have to tell you this because it really shows how bad it's gotten. My supervisor, Kevin—he's this young guy, maybe thirty-five, always wears those tight polo shirts with the company logo—he actually pulled me into his glass office on Wednesday. He had this manila folder sitting right there on his desk, and he told me, to my face, that he’s been documenting problems with my reliability. 

My reliability! Doc, I have never been unreliable a single day in my life. I’ve always been the first one on the floor, ready to pull pallets and fill orders. But because I’m so paralyzed by that blasted computer tablet, I’ve been making mistakes, taking too long, even clocking in late because I've started sitting in my truck in the parking lot for twenty minutes just trying to work up the nerve to walk through the doors. Kevin actually sat there, looked me in the eye, and talked to me about taking medical leave. Medical leave! Like I'm some kind of invalid who can't do a day's work anymore. It just breaks my heart to see everything I've built up—my reputation as a hard worker, my responsibilities at the church—just falling apart on me like this.

I get there early, I hand out the bulletins, I help the older folks find their seats, and I always organize the men's group to set up the folding tables for the monthly pancake breakfast. People really count on me to get all that done, you know? \n\nBut these last few weeks... I just haven't had it in me. I actually called Pastor Miller last week and told him I had to step back from my church duties. I just couldn't face the crowd, couldn't put on a happy face and shake hands when my stomach is tied in absolute knots. Mary went to service by herself this past Sunday, and I just sat in my armchair in the living room feeling terrible about it. I've pulled back from the very people who rely on me, and I hate letting them down.\n\nAnd honestly, it’s getting to the point where folks are noticing at the job, too, which is just the most shameful part of it all. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just pouring my heart out to you about a minute ago, doc, that's exactly what's happening, and it's... well, it's just the most shameful thing in the world to me. I know I just went on a whole tangent about it, but it really bears repeating because it's just eating me alive. 

Take the church again. When I told Pastor Miller I had to step back from my duties, I wasn't just giving up some little Sunday morning chore. I was letting down people who genuinely counted on me. I've been the guy they look to for fifteen years to make sure the service runs smooth, to get the older folks seated safely, to get the tables set up... and now I'm just hiding out at home in my armchair while Mary goes alone. It makes me feel about two inches tall, honestly, to just pull back from my community like that. 

And then, like I was saying about the warehouse... I still can't quite wrap my head around it. The fact that my supervisor, Kevin, has actually documented problems with my reliability. He put it on paper! I've been a stocker and an order filler my whole life, I've worked through terrible colds, I've worked through a sprained ankle back in '98, and I have never, ever been called unreliable. I pride myself on that. But because I can't get my stomach to settle down over that new computer system, I'm messing up. And for him to sit across from his desk and actually talk to me about taking medical leave? Suggesting I need to step away because I can't do a regular day's work anymore? It's humiliating. 

So, yes, to answer your question, my ability to function and do the things people expect of me... it's falling completely apart, both at work and everywhere else.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I suppose I'm here because things have just gotten completely out of hand lately. I've been a stocker over at the warehouse for years, you know, and I always prided myself on being reliable. But then, a few months back, they went and completely overhauled everything. They changed my hours, which was hard enough at my age—I'm 74, I like my routine—but then they brought in this whole new computerized inventory system. And I tell you, it's just... it's like the ground fell out from under me. \n\nEver since then, I just feel like I'm completely falling apart. I can't seem to get a grip on it. It's gotten so bad that I'm actually missing shifts, which I never do. I've even been missing my Sunday commitments at church because I'm just so overwhelmed. And my family... Lord, I've been snapping at my wife and kids over the smallest things, things that never used to bother me. Some mornings, I swear, I just sit there on the edge of the bed, all laced up in my work boots, staring at the wall, and I just wonder if I can even bring myself to walk through those warehouse doors again. It's just too much, doc, it really is. I don't know what's happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like church or time with family?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I've lost interest, no. It's really not like that at all. Take church, for example. I've been going to the same congregation since my wife and I bought our house back in '78, and I usually help usher on the second and fourth Sundays. I love it there, I really do. I haven't lost one bit of interest in it. It's just... well, when Sunday morning rolls around now, my stomach is in absolute knots. I sit down at the kitchen table to eat my toast and drink my coffee, and all I can think about is Monday morning and that glowing scanner screen they expect me to use. My mind just starts spinning about how I'm going to mess up the inventory count again, or press the wrong button and delete a whole pallet's worth of data. \n\nSo it's not that I don't want to be there, seeing everyone and listening to the sermon, it's just that I'm so wound up and plain exhausted from worrying that I just can't bring myself to put on my suit and make small talk. And it's the same with my family. I love my wife dearly, and my kids, they're good kids. I haven't lost interest in them either. If anything, I feel absolutely terrible that I'm snapping at them the way I am. I don't feel hopeless about life in general, you know? I'm not sitting around crying or wanting to give up on everything. I'm just so blasted frustrated and overwhelmed by this one thing at work. I come home and my brain is completely fried from trying to figure out that new software. I just want things to go back to how they were with the paper logs. I used a clipboard for twenty years, doc. Twenty years! I had a whole system with carbon copies, and it worked perfectly fine, never missed a beat... and now it's just all turned upside down.\n\nDoctor: When you think about going into work or using the new inventory system, do you notice any physical reactions, like your heart racing, sweating, or feeling shaky?\n\nPatient: Well, physical reactions... let me think about that. You know, I was just telling my wife the other day—Mary, she's been a saint through all this, really she has—I was telling her that it feels like my body is just rebelling against me. When I'm getting ready for work, putting on my uniform... it's a blue shirt, we've had the same blue shirts since 2005, though they changed the logo a few years back... anyway, when I'm buttoning that shirt, my hands... they don't exactly shake like a leaf, but they just feel clumsy, you know? Like I can't quite get the buttons through the holes the way I used to. \n\nAnd my heart, well, I wouldn't say it's racing like a racehorse or anything like that. It's not pounding out of my chest. I've had my heart checked, Dr. Miller did an EKG on me just last year for my annual physical and said my ticker was strong for a man my age. But it's more like a heaviness, a tightness right here in the center of my chest when I pull into the parking lot. I park in the same spot every day, third row, fourth spot from the left, right under the big oak tree. And when I turn off the ignition, that tightness just settles in. \n\nI don't really get sweaty, no. I've never been much of a sweater, even when I was younger and doing the heavy lifting in the warehouse before they moved me to the lighter aisles. But my stomach, like I mentioned before... it just churns. It's like I've drank a gallon of sour milk. I just sit in my truck for a good ten minutes, staring at the employee entrance, and my stomach is doing flip-flops, just dreading having to log into that blasted tablet. It's an iPad, I think they call it. They gave us all these little styluses to use with them, but my fingers are too thick, and I keep hitting the wrong things on the screen. Just thinking about it now makes my chest feel tight again. So, no shaking or sweating really, just that awful feeling in my gut and that heavy, tight feeling in my chest.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of worry and tightness in your chest happen even when you're not at work or thinking about work, like in the evenings or on days off?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that for a second... I suppose the short answer is no, not really. It's really just tied to that warehouse and that infernal computer system. Like, take last Saturday. Mary and I, we have a routine on Saturdays. We always go to the farmer's market downtown—have been since, oh, maybe 2012? Whenever they moved it from the old fairgrounds to the square. We get our tomatoes from this fellow named Henderson, nice guy, always wears a straw hat. Anyway, we were walking around, picking up some sweet corn, and I felt right as rain. My chest was fine, my stomach was settled, I even had a big old slice of cherry pie from the baker's stall, and it sat perfectly fine. I wasn't worried, I wasn't tight in the chest. I was just enjoying the sunshine and being with my wife.\n\nAnd in the evenings, too, once I'm home and I've had my supper... Mary usually makes meatloaf on Tuesdays, and it's my favorite. Once I'm sitting in my recliner—it's a La-Z-Boy, got it on sale about five years ago, very comfortable—and I turn on the television to watch the evening news or maybe a western, I feel okay. I mean, I might be tired, bone-tired sometimes, because I'm 74 and lifting boxes isn't getting any easier, but that awful, heavy tightness in my chest? No, that's gone. \n\nIt really only starts creeping back in when Sunday evening rolls around, right about the time the sun starts going down. That's when I start thinking, \"Well, tomorrow's Monday. Tomorrow I have to go back and face that iPad thing.\" And then, sure enough, the stomach starts to sour a bit, and by Monday morning when I'm buttoning that blue shirt, the tightness is right back there, sitting on my chest like a sack of flour. But if I'm not thinking about the warehouse, if I'm just living my life, puttering around the garage or watching a ballgame? No, I feel like my old self. It's just that one thing, doc, just that one blasted change they made, and it's ruining the rest of my week.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about other things in your life, not just work, like your health, family responsibilities, or everyday tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Worrying about other things? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. It's really just the job. Take my health, for instance. Like I told you, Dr. Miller gave me a clean bill of health at my last physical. I don't sit around fretting about getting older or getting sick. I figure I've made it to 74, I must be doing something right, you know? I take a daily multivitamin with my breakfast, and that's about the extent of my worrying there. \n\nAnd the family... no, no worries there either. My oldest, David, he's an accountant over in Springfield, doing very well for himself. Got two boys of his own, both in high school now, playing baseball. Good kids. And my daughter Sarah, she's a second-grade teacher. They're all settled, making a good living. I don't lose a single wink of sleep over them, thank the Lord. They check in on us, we see them on holidays, it's all just fine.\n\nAs for the house and everyday chores... well, Mary and I have been in that house since '78, so we've got a system for everything. I mow the lawn every Thursday evening, regular as clockwork. I've got a John Deere riding mower, bought it second-hand from a neighbor—old Mr. Higgins, God rest his soul—about ten years ago. Still runs like a top because I make sure to maintain it. I just get out there, do my laps around the yard, and it's actually real peaceful. It's certainly not something I dread or fret over. Same with fixing things around the house. If a lightbulb goes out or a hinge gets squeaky, I just go to my workbench in the garage, grab my tools, and take care of it. \n\nSo, no, doc, I'm really not a worrier by nature. I never have been. I've always been the kind of guy who just takes things as they come. If it weren't for that blasted tablet and the new software they sprang on us down at the warehouse, I'd be perfectly content. It's just that one single thing that's got my stomach in knots. Everything else in my life is just... well, just normal, everyday life, and I'm perfectly fine with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worry about the new inventory system and your job performance feels uncontrollable or that something bad will happen if you make a mistake with it?\n\nPatient: Uncontrollable? I can control my thoughts just fine when I'm away from the place, sitting in my recliner or out at the market. \n\nAnd as for something bad happening if I make a mistake... well, let me explain how it works down there on the floor. See, if you mess up on the tablet—and I have, I'll be the first to admit it, my fingers just don't glide over that glass screen the way the young kids' do. Just last week, Thursday I think it was, I was trying to log a shipment of those big yellow car-wash sponges we get in from a supplier over in Ohio. Good quality sponges, we've stocked them for years, usually go right to Aisle 7. Anyway, I was supposed to hit \"Received\" on the screen, and I think my knuckle bumped \"Pending\" or something like that. The screen flashed a different color, and I just stood there looking at it, not knowing how to go back. \n\nNow, did I think the warehouse was going to explode? No. Did I think the police were going to show up or I'd be ruined? Course not. The worst that happens is young Greg—he's the floor manager now, nice enough kid, maybe 26, drives a little red Honda Civic that he parks near the dumpsters—Greg just has to come over, sigh a little bit, tap a few buttons, and fix it. He doesn't yell, he just says, \"Let's fix that, Bob, watch how I do it.\" \n\nSo I know I'm not going to get hauled off to jail or fired on the spot, and I know it's not the end of the world. But it's just... it's the embarrassment of it, doc. It's feeling like a dinosaur in a place I used to know like the back of my hand. I'm not terrified of a disaster, I'm just dreading the sheer frustration of knowing I'm going to struggle with the blasted thing every single shift. I hate feeling like I can't do my job the way I've done it perfectly well for two decades. So no, I wouldn't say the worry is out of my control, and I don't think anything terrible is going to happen. It's just a miserable, heavy feeling of not being able to do what I'm supposed to do, and I just hate having to face it.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress about work affected any other parts of your life, like your relationships, your ability to enjoy activities, or your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I hate to admit it, but yes, it actually has started bleeding over into things. It’s embarrassing, really. You know I mentioned young Greg earlier, the floor manager? Well, it’s gotten to the point where he actually had to pull me into that little glass office they have right next to the loading bays. It was a Tuesday, I remember, because the big freight trucks from Chicago always come in on Tuesdays and it’s loud as all get-out in that part of the building. Anyway, I sat down, and he had this manila folder on his desk, and he told me he had to officially document some problems with my reliability. \n\nMe! I haven’t had a reliability issue in twenty years! I've always been the guy who shows up early. But because I’m struggling so much with that tablet, getting so far behind, and having to step away when my chest gets tight just to catch my breath, my numbers are way down. He was real gentle about it, I'll give him that, but he actually suggested I look into taking a medical leave. Medical leave! I’ve never taken a leave in my life, I'm not a fragile guy, but he said I just don't seem like myself and maybe I need time to get things sorted out. \n\nAnd I suppose he’s right, because it is affecting my life outside the warehouse, too. Mary and I, we go to Grace Methodist over on 4th Street. We've been members there since David and Sarah were in Sunday school, and I’ve been the head usher for the 10 AM service for, oh, going on fifteen years now. It’s a real responsibility, you know? You have to coordinate the bulletins, make sure old Mrs. Gable gets her aisle seat because of her walker, organize the collection plates, make sure the microphones are turned on for the choir... people really count on me to make sure the morning runs smooth. \n\nBut lately... I just can't do it. I had to call Pastor Jim just last week and tell him I need to pull back from all my church duties for a while. I felt terrible about it, just awful, leaving them in the lurch like that, especially since they rely on me to lock up the fellowship hall after coffee hour. But by the time Sunday morning rolls around, I’ve already got this heavy dread sitting on me about Monday, and I just can't muster the energy to put on my suit, stand at those double doors, and smile at everybody. I just don't have the spirit for it right now. So, yes, doc, I guess it really is taking a toll on things I used to take a lot of pride in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite related to how you’ve been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep and appetite... well, let me think on that. No, no, I really can't say I've noticed any changes there. \n\nLet me tell you about my routine. Mary and I, we've always been creatures of habit, you see. Every night, after we finish up the dinner dishes—I wash, she dries, that’s just how we’ve always done it—we sit down in the living room. I've got my brown La-Z-Boy, she's got her floral armchair, and we turn on Channel 4 for the local news at 10 o'clock. We like the anchor there, Tom, he’s a straight shooter. Anyway, once the weather report is done, usually right around 10:25, we turn off the set and head up to bed. I'm usually out like a light by 10:45. \n\nNow, sure, at 74 years old, I might have to get up once in the middle of the night to use the restroom, but that's just par for the course, right? I go right back to bed, pull up the covers, and I'm back to snoring. We bought one of those firm mattresses over at the mattress place by the mall about five years ago, and it does wonders for my back. I sleep straight through until my alarm goes off at 6 AM, just like I have for decades. So, no tossing and turning, no lying awake staring at the ceiling. \n\nAnd my appetite, well, that's just fine, too. Mary is a fantastic cook, always has been. Just last night she made her pot roast with the little red potatoes and carrots—the way she slow-cooks it, the meat just falls right apart on your fork. I had two helpings! And this morning, I had my usual: two eggs over easy, two slices of whole-wheat toast with just a little scrape of butter, and a hot cup of black coffee. I've always been a meat-and-potatoes kind of guy, and I still look forward to every meal. \n\nYou’d think with my stomach in knots over that blasted tablet system during my shift, I wouldn't want to eat a thing, but once I'm home and I smell what Mary's got on the stove, I eat just as much as I ever did. So, no, doc, eating and sleeping are about the only things that still feel completely normal these days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you aren’t getting as much pleasure out of things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Pleasure out of things... well, let me think on that for a second. No, I wouldn't say that's happening. No. \n\nBecause, see, when I'm not at that warehouse, or when I'm not sitting there dreading going *to* the warehouse, I'm still me. I still like what I like. Take my garden, for example. Now, Mary and I have lived in our house on Maple Drive since 1978. Bought it for a song back then, if you can believe it, a nice little three-bedroom ranch. And out back, right past the patio, I've got this vegetable garden. I've been tending that exact same patch of dirt for over forty years. \n\nJust this past Saturday, I was out there. The sun was shining, it was maybe about 70 degrees, just perfect weather, really, not too humid yet. I was out there tying up the tomato plants to the stakes—I always grow the Big Boy tomatoes, plus a few rows of those little cherry tomatoes for the grandkids, because they just love to pluck them right off the vine and pop them in their mouths when they come over. And I tell you, doc, getting my hands down in that soil, smelling the damp earth after Mary watered it the night before... it feels just as good as it always has. I still get a real kick out of seeing those first little green tomatoes start to pop up on the vines. I can spend three, four hours out there and lose all track of time, just weeding and pruning, and I enjoy every single minute of it. \n\nAnd then in the afternoons, I'll go into the garage. I've got this old 1968 Ford pickup I've been tinkering with since I bought it off my brother-in-law back in '95. Nothing major, just keeping her running, polishing the chrome, cleaning the carburetor. I can pull up my stool out there, turn on the radio—usually I listen to the oldies station, they play all the good stuff from the sixties and seventies, none of this new noise—and I'm as happy as a clam. \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost my enjoyment of things. When I'm actually able to put work out of my mind—which, granted, is getting harder and harder to do once Sunday afternoon rolls around—but when I'm just living my life, doing my hobbies, I still enjoy them just as much as I did ten, twenty years ago. The joy is still there. It's just that blasted inventory tablet hanging over my head the rest of the time that makes me miserable. But my garden and my truck? No, those are still my pride and joy.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that even when you're doing the things you enjoy, like gardening or working on your truck, they don't feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doc, like I was just saying, it’s really not like that at all. Maybe I didn't explain it right, but when I'm out there doing my things, the satisfaction is a hundred percent still there. \n\nTake the truck, for instance. Just this past Sunday afternoon—this was right before the Sunday evening dread really started creeping in on me, you know—I was out in the garage with a microfiber rag and a tin of that Turtle Wax. You know the kind in the little green tub? Smells a bit strong, like chemicals, but I tell you, it gets a shine on old chrome like nothing else on the market. I spent a good hour just buffing the front bumper of that '68 Ford. Just rubbing it in little circles, focusing on the metal, listening to the oldies station. \n\nAnd when I finally stepped back and wiped away the haze, and I could see my own reflection clear as day in that bumper... well, I felt real proud. Real satisfied, you know? It felt just as good as it did the day I bought it from my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary took decent care of it, sure, but I’ve always kept it pristine. \n\nSo, no, the pleasure isn't gone one bit. The activities themselves feel exactly the same, they're my refuge, really. It’s just getting my mind off the warehouse and that blasted tablet long enough to go out and do them that's the trick. But once I'm actually doing them? I still love every single second of it. If I lost the joy in my truck or my tomatoes, well, I don't know what I'd do, but thank the good Lord that hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about work has started to generalize to other areas of your life, even those that used to feel safe or predictable?\n\nPatient: Generalize? You mean, am I getting all worked up and nervous about other things, like my regular day-to-day stuff? Well, let me think on that for a second... no. No, I really don't think so. It’s strictly the warehouse. \n\nBecause, you see, everything else in my life makes sense. Everything else is just the way it’s always been. Take Sunday mornings, for example. You’d think with me being so tied up in knots over that new computer tablet at work, I might be getting nervous about other kinds of paperwork or numbers, right? But no, not at all. Every Sunday morning, right after Mary and I finish our coffee—we still use this old stainless steel percolator we got for our twenty-fifth wedding anniversary, makes a much better, hotter cup than those new plastic drip machines they sell nowadays—we sit down at the kitchen table. I get out the checkbook, the one with the blue leather cover, and I go through the mail. \n\nI open up the bills—the electric, the water, the property tax when it comes around in the fall. I sit there with my favorite ballpoint pen, the metal one I got from the bank years ago, and I write out every single check by hand. I balance the ledger down to the very last penny. I’ve done it exactly that way since, oh, probably 1974. And I don't feel an ounce of worry when I do it. I don't get sweaty palms or a racing heart. It's safe, it's predictable, and most importantly, it makes sense to me. I'm in control of it. \n\nSame goes for just running errands. I drive down to the Ace Hardware on Main Street to pick up fertilizer or a new spark plug, and I know exactly where everything is in that store. I know the owner, Bill, we talk about the weather for ten minutes, I pay with cash, and I drive home. It’s just easy. Or mowing the lawn! I've got this Toro push-mower, gas-powered. You prime it three times, pull the cord, and it starts. Every time. There are no passwords to remember, no error screens flashing at you, no supervisor breathing down your neck telling you you scanned the wrong barcode on a pallet of dog food. \n\nSo, no, doc, the rest of my life is perfectly fine. The anxiety hasn't bled over into my home or my marriage or my chores. It's just that one specific place, that warehouse, and that one specific machine that's ruining my peace of mind. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me, going about my business exactly the same way I always have.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you put it that way... I suppose, yes, there have been some things slipping. It's hard to admit, because like I said, I like to keep things orderly and I pride myself on being a dependable guy. But take my church, for instance. Mary and I have been going to First Methodist down on 4th Street for over forty years. And for the last fifteen of those years, I’ve been the head usher for the 9 AM service. I get there early, I hand out the bulletins, I help the older folks find their seats, and I always organize the men's group to set up the folding tables for the monthly pancake breakfast. People really count on me to get all that done, you know? \n\nBut these last few weeks... I just haven't had it in me. I actually called Pastor Miller last week and told him I had to step back from my church duties. I just couldn't face the crowd, couldn't put on a happy face and shake hands when my stomach is tied in absolute knots. Mary went to service by herself this past Sunday, and I just sat in my armchair in the living room feeling terrible about it. I've pulled back from the very people who rely on me, and I hate letting them down.\n\nAnd honestly, it’s getting to the point where folks are noticing at the job, too, which is just the most shameful part of it all. I know you asked about things outside of work, but I have to tell you this because it really shows how bad it's gotten. My supervisor, Kevin—he's this young guy, maybe thirty-five, always wears those tight polo shirts with the company logo—he actually pulled me into his glass office on Wednesday. He had this manila folder sitting right there on his desk, and he told me, to my face, that he’s been documenting problems with my reliability. \n\nMy reliability! Doc, I have never been unreliable a single day in my life. I’ve always been the first one on the floor, ready to pull pallets and fill orders. But because I’m so paralyzed by that blasted computer tablet, I’ve been making mistakes, taking too long, even clocking in late because I've started sitting in my truck in the parking lot for twenty minutes just trying to work up the nerve to walk through the doors. Kevin actually sat there, looked me in the eye, and talked to me about taking medical leave. Medical leave! Like I'm some kind of invalid who can't do a day's work anymore. It just breaks my heart to see everything I've built up—my reputation as a hard worker, my responsibilities at the church—just falling apart on me like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just pouring my heart out to you about a minute ago, doc, that's exactly what's happening, and it's... well, it's just the most shameful thing in the world to me. I know I just went on a whole tangent about it, but it really bears repeating because it's just eating me alive. \n\nTake the church again. When I told Pastor Miller I had to step back from my duties, I wasn't just giving up some little Sunday morning chore. I was letting down people who genuinely counted on me. I've been the guy they look to for fifteen years to make sure the service runs smooth, to get the older folks seated safely, to get the tables set up... and now I'm just hiding out at home in my armchair while Mary goes alone. It makes me feel about two inches tall, honestly, to just pull back from my community like that. \n\nAnd then, like I was saying about the warehouse... I still can't quite wrap my head around it. The fact that my supervisor, Kevin, has actually documented problems with my reliability. He put it on paper! I've been a stocker and an order filler my whole life, I've worked through terrible colds, I've worked through a sprained ankle back in '98, and I have never, ever been called unreliable. I pride myself on that. But because I can't get my stomach to settle down over that new computer system, I'm messing up. And for him to sit across from his desk and actually talk to me about taking medical leave? Suggesting I need to step away because I can't do a regular day's work anymore? It's humiliating. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question, my ability to function and do the things people expect of me... it's falling completely apart, both at work and everywhere else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’ve done it exactly that way since, oh, probably 1974. And I don't feel an ounce of worry when I do it. I don't get sweaty palms or a racing heart. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was trying to explain earlier, doc... no, not really. The anxiety, the feeling on edge, the heart racing—it's strictly tied to that warehouse and that blasted tablet. It's funny you ask that again, because I really have been thinking about it, turning it over in my mind, trying to see if maybe I am just a nervous wreck all over. But I'm not. 

I mean, take my hobbies. I didn't mention this before, but I've been building model airplanes since I was a teenager. Specifically, World War II fighters—P-51 Mustangs, Spitfires, Corsairs. I have a whole workbench set up in the basement, right next to the old chest freezer. I've got my magnifying glass on a little swivel arm, my tiny little paintbrushes, those little jars of enamel paint that smell like turpentine, and my tweezers. I can sit down there for three, sometimes four hours on a Saturday afternoon, just carefully gluing the landing gear onto a 1/48 scale model. You need an incredibly steady hand for that. If I were on edge, if my nerves were shot, I'd be getting glue all over the canopy, or I wouldn't be able to paint the little instrument panels. But my hands are steady as a rock down there. I put on the classic rock station, maybe sip a glass of iced tea, and I am completely relaxed. It's my little sanctuary. 

And it's the same with the errands, like I mentioned with the hardware store. Or even just going to the grocery store with Mary. We go to the Piggly Wiggly over on the east side of town every Thursday morning. We always get a cart with a wonky wheel, it seems like, but we just go up and down the aisles, picking out our usual things—the store-brand oatmeal, a couple of pounds of ground chuck for meatloaf, some iceberg lettuce. I push the cart, I chat with the checkout girl, Brenda—she's been there for years, her daughter just graduated high school, actually—and I don't feel a lick of panic. None of that tight feeling in my chest. 

So, it's just so frustrating, you see? Because I know how to be calm. I know how to relax in my basement, or at the grocery store, or paying my bills. It's just when I think about that warehouse, or when I pull into that parking lot, that everything goes haywire. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me.

Maybe I didn't explain it right, but when I'm out there doing my things, the satisfaction is a hundred percent still there. \n\nTake the truck, for instance. Just this past Sunday afternoon—this was right before the Sunday evening dread really started creeping in on me, you know—I was out in the garage with a microfiber rag and a tin of that Turtle Wax. You know the kind in the little green tub? Smells a bit strong, like chemicals, but I tell you, it gets a shine on old chrome like nothing else on the market. I spent a good hour just buffing the front bumper of that '68 Ford. Just rubbing it in little circles, focusing on the metal, listening to the oldies station. \n\nAnd when I finally stepped back and wiped away the haze, and I could see my own reflection clear as day in that bumper... well, I felt real proud. Real satisfied, you know? It felt just as good as it did the day I bought it from my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary took decent care of it, sure, but I’ve always kept it pristine. \n\nSo, no, the pleasure isn't gone one bit. The activities themselves feel exactly the same, they're my refuge, really. It’s just getting my mind off the warehouse and that blasted tablet long enough to go out and do them that's the trick. But once I'm actually doing them? I still love every single second of it. If I lost the joy in my truck or my tomatoes, well, I don't know what I'd do, but thank the good Lord that hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about work has started to generalize to other areas of your life, even those that used to feel safe or predictable?\n\nPatient: Generalize? You mean, am I getting all worked up and nervous about other things, like my regular day-to-day stuff? Well, let me think on that for a second... no. No, I really don't think so. It’s strictly the warehouse. \n\nBecause, you see, everything else in my life makes sense. Everything else is just the way it’s always been. Take Sunday mornings, for example. You’d think with me being so tied up in knots over that new computer tablet at work, I might be getting nervous about other kinds of paperwork or numbers, right? But no, not at all. Every Sunday morning, right after Mary and I finish our coffee—we still use this old stainless steel percolator we got for our twenty-fifth wedding anniversary, makes a much better, hotter cup than those new plastic drip machines they sell nowadays—we sit down at the kitchen table. I get out the checkbook, the one with the blue leather cover, and I go through the mail. \n\nI open up the bills—the electric, the water, the property tax when it comes around in the fall. I sit there with my favorite ballpoint pen, the metal one I got from the bank years ago, and I write out every single check by hand. I balance the ledger down to the very last penny. I’ve done it exactly that way since, oh, probably 1974. And I don't feel an ounce of worry when I do it. I don't get sweaty palms or a racing heart. It's safe, it's predictable, and most importantly, it makes sense to me. I'm in control of it. \n\nSame goes for just running errands. I drive down to the Ace Hardware on Main Street to pick up fertilizer or a new spark plug, and I know exactly where everything is in that store. I know the owner, Bill, we talk about the weather for ten minutes, I pay with cash, and I drive home. It’s just easy. Or mowing the lawn! I've got this Toro push-mower, gas-powered. You prime it three times, pull the cord, and it starts. Every time. There are no passwords to remember, no error screens flashing at you, no supervisor breathing down your neck telling you you scanned the wrong barcode on a pallet of dog food. \n\nSo, no, doc, the rest of my life is perfectly fine. The anxiety hasn't bled over into my home or my marriage or my chores. It's just that one specific place, that warehouse, and that one specific machine that's ruining my peace of mind. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me, going about my business exactly the same way I always have.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you put it that way... I suppose, yes, there have been some things slipping. It's hard to admit, because like I said, I like to keep things orderly and I pride myself on being a dependable guy. But take my church, for instance. Mary and I have been going to First Methodist down on 4th Street for over forty years. And for the last fifteen of those years, I’ve been the head usher for the 9 AM service. I get there early, I hand out the bulletins, I help the older folks find their seats, and I always organize the men's group to set up the folding tables for the monthly pancake breakfast. People really count on me to get all that done, you know? \n\nBut these last few weeks... I just haven't had it in me. I actually called Pastor Miller last week and told him I had to step back from my church duties. I just couldn't face the crowd, couldn't put on a happy face and shake hands when my stomach is tied in absolute knots. Mary went to service by herself this past Sunday, and I just sat in my armchair in the living room feeling terrible about it. I've pulled back from the very people who rely on me, and I hate letting them down.\n\nAnd honestly, it’s getting to the point where folks are noticing at the job, too, which is just the most shameful part of it all. I know you asked about things outside of work, but I have to tell you this because it really shows how bad it's gotten. My supervisor, Kevin—he's this young guy, maybe thirty-five, always wears those tight polo shirts with the company logo—he actually pulled me into his glass office on Wednesday. He had this manila folder sitting right there on his desk, and he told me, to my face, that he’s been documenting problems with my reliability. \n\nMy reliability! Doc, I have never been unreliable a single day in my life. I’ve always been the first one on the floor, ready to pull pallets and fill orders. But because I’m so paralyzed by that blasted computer tablet, I’ve been making mistakes, taking too long, even clocking in late because I've started sitting in my truck in the parking lot for twenty minutes just trying to work up the nerve to walk through the doors. Kevin actually sat there, looked me in the eye, and talked to me about taking medical leave. Medical leave! Like I'm some kind of invalid who can't do a day's work anymore. It just breaks my heart to see everything I've built up—my reputation as a hard worker, my responsibilities at the church—just falling apart on me like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just pouring my heart out to you about a minute ago, doc, that's exactly what's happening, and it's... well, it's just the most shameful thing in the world to me. I know I just went on a whole tangent about it, but it really bears repeating because it's just eating me alive. \n\nTake the church again. When I told Pastor Miller I had to step back from my duties, I wasn't just giving up some little Sunday morning chore. I was letting down people who genuinely counted on me. I've been the guy they look to for fifteen years to make sure the service runs smooth, to get the older folks seated safely, to get the tables set up... and now I'm just hiding out at home in my armchair while Mary goes alone. It makes me feel about two inches tall, honestly, to just pull back from my community like that. \n\nAnd then, like I was saying about the warehouse... I still can't quite wrap my head around it. The fact that my supervisor, Kevin, has actually documented problems with my reliability. He put it on paper! I've been a stocker and an order filler my whole life, I've worked through terrible colds, I've worked through a sprained ankle back in '98, and I have never, ever been called unreliable. I pride myself on that. But because I can't get my stomach to settle down over that new computer system, I'm messing up. And for him to sit across from his desk and actually talk to me about taking medical leave? Suggesting I need to step away because I can't do a regular day's work anymore? It's humiliating. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question, my ability to function and do the things people expect of me... it's falling completely apart, both at work and everywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety symptoms, like feeling on edge or having trouble relaxing, in situations outside of work, such as at home, during errands, or while participating in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain earlier, doc... no, not really. The anxiety, the feeling on edge, the heart racing—it's strictly tied to that warehouse and that blasted tablet. It's funny you ask that again, because I really have been thinking about it, turning it over in my mind, trying to see if maybe I am just a nervous wreck all over. But I'm not. \n\nI mean, take my hobbies. I didn't mention this before, but I've been building model airplanes since I was a teenager. Specifically, World War II fighters—P-51 Mustangs, Spitfires, Corsairs. I have a whole workbench set up in the basement, right next to the old chest freezer. I've got my magnifying glass on a little swivel arm, my tiny little paintbrushes, those little jars of enamel paint that smell like turpentine, and my tweezers. I can sit down there for three, sometimes four hours on a Saturday afternoon, just carefully gluing the landing gear onto a 1/48 scale model. You need an incredibly steady hand for that. If I were on edge, if my nerves were shot, I'd be getting glue all over the canopy, or I wouldn't be able to paint the little instrument panels. But my hands are steady as a rock down there. I put on the classic rock station, maybe sip a glass of iced tea, and I am completely relaxed. It's my little sanctuary. \n\nAnd it's the same with the errands, like I mentioned with the hardware store. Or even just going to the grocery store with Mary. We go to the Piggly Wiggly over on the east side of town every Thursday morning. We always get a cart with a wonky wheel, it seems like, but we just go up and down the aisles, picking out our usual things—the store-brand oatmeal, a couple of pounds of ground chuck for meatloaf, some iceberg lettuce. I push the cart, I chat with the checkout girl, Brenda—she's been there for years, her daughter just graduated high school, actually—and I don't feel a lick of panic. None of that tight feeling in my chest. \n\nSo, it's just so frustrating, you see? Because I know how to be calm. I know how to relax in my basement, or at the grocery store, or paying my bills. It's just when I think about that warehouse, or when I pull into that parking lot, that everything goes haywire. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

So, no, to answer your question, I'm not constantly thinking about my performance when I'm at home. That's why it's so baffling. I can completely turn it off when I'm in my own space.

I drive down to the Ace Hardware on Main Street to pick up fertilizer or a new spark plug, and I know exactly where everything is in that store. I know the owner, Bill, we talk about the weather for ten minutes, I pay with cash, and I drive home. It’s just easy. Or mowing the lawn! I've got this Toro push-mower, gas-powered. You prime it three times, pull the cord, and it starts. Every time. There are no passwords to remember, no error screens flashing at you, no supervisor breathing down your neck telling you you scanned the wrong barcode on a pallet of dog food. \n\nSo, no, doc, the rest of my life is perfectly fine. The anxiety hasn't bled over into my home or my marriage or my chores. It's just that one specific place, that warehouse, and that one specific machine that's ruining my peace of mind. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me, going about my business exactly the same way I always have.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you put it that way... I suppose, yes, there have been some things slipping. It's hard to admit, because like I said, I like to keep things orderly and I pride myself on being a dependable guy. But take my church, for instance. Mary and I have been going to First Methodist down on 4th Street for over forty years. And for the last fifteen of those years, I’ve been the head usher for the 9 AM service. I get there early, I hand out the bulletins, I help the older folks find their seats, and I always organize the men's group to set up the folding tables for the monthly pancake breakfast. People really count on me to get all that done, you know? \n\nBut these last few weeks... I just haven't had it in me. I actually called Pastor Miller last week and told him I had to step back from my church duties. I just couldn't face the crowd, couldn't put on a happy face and shake hands when my stomach is tied in absolute knots. Mary went to service by herself this past Sunday, and I just sat in my armchair in the living room feeling terrible about it. I've pulled back from the very people who rely on me, and I hate letting them down.\n\nAnd honestly, it’s getting to the point where folks are noticing at the job, too, which is just the most shameful part of it all. I know you asked about things outside of work, but I have to tell you this because it really shows how bad it's gotten. My supervisor, Kevin—he's this young guy, maybe thirty-five, always wears those tight polo shirts with the company logo—he actually pulled me into his glass office on Wednesday. He had this manila folder sitting right there on his desk, and he told me, to my face, that he’s been documenting problems with my reliability. \n\nMy reliability! Doc, I have never been unreliable a single day in my life. I’ve always been the first one on the floor, ready to pull pallets and fill orders. But because I’m so paralyzed by that blasted computer tablet, I’ve been making mistakes, taking too long, even clocking in late because I've started sitting in my truck in the parking lot for twenty minutes just trying to work up the nerve to walk through the doors. Kevin actually sat there, looked me in the eye, and talked to me about taking medical leave. Medical leave! Like I'm some kind of invalid who can't do a day's work anymore. It just breaks my heart to see everything I've built up—my reputation as a hard worker, my responsibilities at the church—just falling apart on me like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just pouring my heart out to you about a minute ago, doc, that's exactly what's happening, and it's... well, it's just the most shameful thing in the world to me. I know I just went on a whole tangent about it, but it really bears repeating because it's just eating me alive. \n\nTake the church again. When I told Pastor Miller I had to step back from my duties, I wasn't just giving up some little Sunday morning chore. I was letting down people who genuinely counted on me. I've been the guy they look to for fifteen years to make sure the service runs smooth, to get the older folks seated safely, to get the tables set up... and now I'm just hiding out at home in my armchair while Mary goes alone. It makes me feel about two inches tall, honestly, to just pull back from my community like that. \n\nAnd then, like I was saying about the warehouse... I still can't quite wrap my head around it. The fact that my supervisor, Kevin, has actually documented problems with my reliability. He put it on paper! I've been a stocker and an order filler my whole life, I've worked through terrible colds, I've worked through a sprained ankle back in '98, and I have never, ever been called unreliable. I pride myself on that. But because I can't get my stomach to settle down over that new computer system, I'm messing up. And for him to sit across from his desk and actually talk to me about taking medical leave? Suggesting I need to step away because I can't do a regular day's work anymore? It's humiliating. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question, my ability to function and do the things people expect of me... it's falling completely apart, both at work and everywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety symptoms, like feeling on edge or having trouble relaxing, in situations outside of work, such as at home, during errands, or while participating in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain earlier, doc... no, not really. The anxiety, the feeling on edge, the heart racing—it's strictly tied to that warehouse and that blasted tablet. It's funny you ask that again, because I really have been thinking about it, turning it over in my mind, trying to see if maybe I am just a nervous wreck all over. But I'm not. \n\nI mean, take my hobbies. I didn't mention this before, but I've been building model airplanes since I was a teenager. Specifically, World War II fighters—P-51 Mustangs, Spitfires, Corsairs. I have a whole workbench set up in the basement, right next to the old chest freezer. I've got my magnifying glass on a little swivel arm, my tiny little paintbrushes, those little jars of enamel paint that smell like turpentine, and my tweezers. I can sit down there for three, sometimes four hours on a Saturday afternoon, just carefully gluing the landing gear onto a 1/48 scale model. You need an incredibly steady hand for that. If I were on edge, if my nerves were shot, I'd be getting glue all over the canopy, or I wouldn't be able to paint the little instrument panels. But my hands are steady as a rock down there. I put on the classic rock station, maybe sip a glass of iced tea, and I am completely relaxed. It's my little sanctuary. \n\nAnd it's the same with the errands, like I mentioned with the hardware store. Or even just going to the grocery store with Mary. We go to the Piggly Wiggly over on the east side of town every Thursday morning. We always get a cart with a wonky wheel, it seems like, but we just go up and down the aisles, picking out our usual things—the store-brand oatmeal, a couple of pounds of ground chuck for meatloaf, some iceberg lettuce. I push the cart, I chat with the checkout girl, Brenda—she's been there for years, her daughter just graduated high school, actually—and I don't feel a lick of panic. None of that tight feeling in my chest. \n\nSo, it's just so frustrating, you see? Because I know how to be calm. I know how to relax in my basement, or at the grocery store, or paying my bills. It's just when I think about that warehouse, or when I pull into that parking lot, that everything goes haywire. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about work-related mistakes or performance, even when you're not at work or around the inventory system?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because... no. No, I really don't. And I think that's what makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating for me to wrap my head around. Because if I were just, you know, losing my marbles or turning into a worrywart in my old age, you'd think I'd be obsessing over it twenty-four seven. You'd think I'd be pacing the floorboards. But I'm not. Once I pull out of that warehouse parking lot—well, after I've sat there in the truck for a minute to catch my breath—and I make that right turn onto Highway 41 to head home, it's like I leave it all in the rearview mirror. \n\nBy the time I pull into my driveway, I'm just me again. Mary and I have our evening routine, you see, and we stick to it. We always eat supper around 5:30. She makes a wonderful pot roast, or maybe we heat up some of that leftover meatloaf I was telling you about from the Piggly Wiggly. We sit down in the living room, we watch the local news on channel 5, and then it's Wheel of Fortune and Jeopardy. We've been doing that for decades, never miss a night if we can help it. And I'll tell you, when I'm sitting in my brown La-Z-Boy, trying to guess the word puzzles or figure out who the 14th president was, my mind is a million miles away from inventory logs and barcodes. \n\nI'm not sitting there agonizing over whether I tapped the wrong button on that screen or if Kevin is sitting in his glass office reviewing my file. I don't chew my fingernails wondering if I put a pallet of dog food in the wrong bay. I'm just enjoying my evening with my wife. I mean, sure, Sunday night rolls around and I might get a little sour feeling in my gut knowing Monday morning is coming, but during the week? When I'm off the clock? No, I don't dwell on my mistakes at all. I know how to do my job, doc, I've done it for fifty years and I know I'm a good worker. \n\nI even sleep like a log most nights. I just close my eyes and I'm out, right up until my alarm goes off at 4:30 AM and reality hits me that I have to go back and face that machine again. So, no, to answer your question, I'm not constantly thinking about my performance when I'm at home. That's why it's so baffling. I can completely turn it off when I'm in my own space.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, the thing about that is... no, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I know exactly what happens when you make a mistake in a warehouse. I've been doing this for, what, fifty years? I started back when everything was done on paper manifests, with carbon copies. You had a pink slip, a yellow slip, and the white original on a heavy clipboard. If you wrote down the wrong aisle number—say you put aisle 4 instead of aisle 14—well, somebody would go looking for the pallet, they wouldn't find it, they'd curse a little bit, and then we'd just track it down and fix the paperwork with a red pen. You initialed it, and that was that. It wasn't the end of the world. 

And it's the same today, really, even with all the technology. I mean, just last spring, we had this young kid, Tyler I think his name was, barely out of high school. He was driving the forklift and he misjudged his turning radius, clipped the edge of a rack, and brought down a whole pallet of those glass mason jars we stock for canning season. I'm telling you, it sounded like a bomb went off in there! Glass everywhere. But you know what happened? We got the push brooms, we swept it up, Kevin printed out a damage report, and Tyler went right back to work. Nobody called the National Guard, nobody lost their house over it. 

So, no, I don't sit there worrying that if I tap the wrong little square on that tablet, the whole company is going to go bankrupt or something. I know logically that if I mess up a barcode scan, it just means the inventory count is off by one until the monthly audit catches it. I know the consequences of a small mistake are just that—small. That's not what's bothering me. 

It's not a fear of... of some grand catastrophe. It's just the sheer frustration of not being able to do it right in the first place, knowing I'm messing up the simple things, and the way my stomach just completely drops when I have to hold that machine. But as for worrying that a little mistake is going to have some serious, life-altering consequence? No, doc, I've seen way too many mistakes in my time to think like that. You just sweep up the glass and move on.
