You’d think with me being so tied up in knots over that new computer tablet at work, I might be getting nervous about other kinds of paperwork or numbers, right? But no, not at all. Every Sunday morning, right after Mary and I finish our coffee—we still use this old stainless steel percolator we got for our twenty-fifth wedding anniversary, makes a much better, hotter cup than those new plastic drip machines they sell nowadays—we sit down at the kitchen table. I get out the checkbook, the one with the blue leather cover, and I go through the mail. \n\nI open up the bills—the electric, the water, the property tax when it comes around in the fall. I sit there with my favorite ballpoint pen, the metal one I got from the bank years ago, and I write out every single check by hand. I balance the ledger down to the very last penny. I’ve done it exactly that way since, oh, probably 1974. And I don't feel an ounce of worry when I do it. I don't get sweaty palms or a racing heart. It's safe, it's predictable, and most importantly, it makes sense to me. I'm in control of it. \n\nSame goes for just running errands. I drive down to the Ace Hardware on Main Street to pick up fertilizer or a new spark plug, and I know exactly where everything is in that store. I know the owner, Bill, we talk about the weather for ten minutes, I pay with cash, and I drive home. It’s just easy. Or mowing the lawn! I've got this Toro push-mower, gas-powered. You prime it three times, pull the cord, and it starts. Every time. There are no passwords to remember, no error screens flashing at you, no supervisor breathing down your neck telling you you scanned the wrong barcode on a pallet of dog food. \n\nSo, no, doc, the rest of my life is perfectly fine. The anxiety hasn't bled over into my home or my marriage or my chores. It's just that one specific place, that warehouse, and that one specific machine that's ruining my peace of mind. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me, going about my business exactly the same way I always have.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you put it that way... I suppose, yes, there have been some things slipping. It's hard to admit, because like I said, I like to keep things orderly and I pride myself on being a dependable guy. But take my church, for instance. Mary and I have been going to First Methodist down on 4th Street for over forty years. And for the last fifteen of those years, I’ve been the head usher for the 9 AM service. I get there early, I hand out the bulletins, I help the older folks find their seats, and I always organize the men's group to set up the folding tables for the monthly pancake breakfast. People really count on me to get all that done, you know? \n\nBut these last few weeks... I just haven't had it in me. I actually called Pastor Miller last week and told him I had to step back from my church duties. I just couldn't face the crowd, couldn't put on a happy face and shake hands when my stomach is tied in absolute knots. Mary went to service by herself this past Sunday, and I just sat in my armchair in the living room feeling terrible about it. I've pulled back from the very people who rely on me, and I hate letting them down.\n\nAnd honestly, it’s getting to the point where folks are noticing at the job, too, which is just the most shameful part of it all. I know you asked about things outside of work, but I have to tell you this because it really shows how bad it's gotten. My supervisor, Kevin—he's this young guy, maybe thirty-five, always wears those tight polo shirts with the company logo—he actually pulled me into his glass office on Wednesday. He had this manila folder sitting right there on his desk, and he told me, to my face, that he’s been documenting problems with my reliability. \n\nMy reliability! Doc, I have never been unreliable a single day in my life. I’ve always been the first one on the floor, ready to pull pallets and fill orders. But because I’m so paralyzed by that blasted computer tablet, I’ve been making mistakes, taking too long, even clocking in late because I've started sitting in my truck in the parking lot for twenty minutes just trying to work up the nerve to walk through the doors. Kevin actually sat there, looked me in the eye, and talked to me about taking medical leave. Medical leave! Like I'm some kind of invalid who can't do a day's work anymore. It just breaks my heart to see everything I've built up—my reputation as a hard worker, my responsibilities at the church—just falling apart on me like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to function in other important areas of your life, like taking care of personal responsibilities, participating in your usual community activities, or maintaining relationships, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just pouring my heart out to you about a minute ago, doc, that's exactly what's happening, and it's... well, it's just the most shameful thing in the world to me. I know I just went on a whole tangent about it, but it really bears repeating because it's just eating me alive. \n\nTake the church again. When I told Pastor Miller I had to step back from my duties, I wasn't just giving up some little Sunday morning chore. I was letting down people who genuinely counted on me. I've been the guy they look to for fifteen years to make sure the service runs smooth, to get the older folks seated safely, to get the tables set up... and now I'm just hiding out at home in my armchair while Mary goes alone. It makes me feel about two inches tall, honestly, to just pull back from my community like that. \n\nAnd then, like I was saying about the warehouse... I still can't quite wrap my head around it. The fact that my supervisor, Kevin, has actually documented problems with my reliability. He put it on paper! I've been a stocker and an order filler my whole life, I've worked through terrible colds, I've worked through a sprained ankle back in '98, and I have never, ever been called unreliable. I pride myself on that. But because I can't get my stomach to settle down over that new computer system, I'm messing up. And for him to sit across from his desk and actually talk to me about taking medical leave? Suggesting I need to step away because I can't do a regular day's work anymore? It's humiliating. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question, my ability to function and do the things people expect of me... it's falling completely apart, both at work and everywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety symptoms, like feeling on edge or having trouble relaxing, in situations outside of work, such as at home, during errands, or while participating in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was trying to explain earlier, doc... no, not really. The anxiety, the feeling on edge, the heart racing—it's strictly tied to that warehouse and that blasted tablet. It's funny you ask that again, because I really have been thinking about it, turning it over in my mind, trying to see if maybe I am just a nervous wreck all over. But I'm not. \n\nI mean, take my hobbies. I didn't mention this before, but I've been building model airplanes since I was a teenager. Specifically, World War II fighters—P-51 Mustangs, Spitfires, Corsairs. I have a whole workbench set up in the basement, right next to the old chest freezer. I've got my magnifying glass on a little swivel arm, my tiny little paintbrushes, those little jars of enamel paint that smell like turpentine, and my tweezers. I can sit down there for three, sometimes four hours on a Saturday afternoon, just carefully gluing the landing gear onto a 1/48 scale model. You need an incredibly steady hand for that. If I were on edge, if my nerves were shot, I'd be getting glue all over the canopy, or I wouldn't be able to paint the little instrument panels. But my hands are steady as a rock down there. I put on the classic rock station, maybe sip a glass of iced tea, and I am completely relaxed. It's my little sanctuary. \n\nAnd it's the same with the errands, like I mentioned with the hardware store. Or even just going to the grocery store with Mary. We go to the Piggly Wiggly over on the east side of town every Thursday morning. We always get a cart with a wonky wheel, it seems like, but we just go up and down the aisles, picking out our usual things—the store-brand oatmeal, a couple of pounds of ground chuck for meatloaf, some iceberg lettuce. I push the cart, I chat with the checkout girl, Brenda—she's been there for years, her daughter just graduated high school, actually—and I don't feel a lick of panic. None of that tight feeling in my chest. \n\nSo, it's just so frustrating, you see? Because I know how to be calm. I know how to relax in my basement, or at the grocery store, or paying my bills. It's just when I think about that warehouse, or when I pull into that parking lot, that everything goes haywire. Everywhere else, I'm just regular old me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about work-related mistakes or performance, even when you're not at work or around the inventory system?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because... no. No, I really don't. And I think that's what makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating for me to wrap my head around. Because if I were just, you know, losing my marbles or turning into a worrywart in my old age, you'd think I'd be obsessing over it twenty-four seven. You'd think I'd be pacing the floorboards. But I'm not. Once I pull out of that warehouse parking lot—well, after I've sat there in the truck for a minute to catch my breath—and I make that right turn onto Highway 41 to head home, it's like I leave it all in the rearview mirror. \n\nBy the time I pull into my driveway, I'm just me again. Mary and I have our evening routine, you see, and we stick to it. We always eat supper around 5:30. She makes a wonderful pot roast, or maybe we heat up some of that leftover meatloaf I was telling you about from the Piggly Wiggly. We sit down in the living room, we watch the local news on channel 5, and then it's Wheel of Fortune and Jeopardy. We've been doing that for decades, never miss a night if we can help it. And I'll tell you, when I'm sitting in my brown La-Z-Boy, trying to guess the word puzzles or figure out who the 14th president was, my mind is a million miles away from inventory logs and barcodes. \n\nI'm not sitting there agonizing over whether I tapped the wrong button on that screen or if Kevin is sitting in his glass office reviewing my file. I don't chew my fingernails wondering if I put a pallet of dog food in the wrong bay. I'm just enjoying my evening with my wife. I mean, sure, Sunday night rolls around and I might get a little sour feeling in my gut knowing Monday morning is coming, but during the week? When I'm off the clock? No, I don't dwell on my mistakes at all. I know how to do my job, doc, I've done it for fifty years and I know I'm a good worker. \n\nI even sleep like a log most nights. I just close my eyes and I'm out, right up until my alarm goes off at 4:30 AM and reality hits me that I have to go back and face that machine again. So, no, to answer your question, I'm not constantly thinking about my performance when I'm at home. That's why it's so baffling. I can completely turn it off when I'm in my own space.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying that even small mistakes at work will have serious consequences, even if you know logically that they won't?\n\nPatient: Well, see, the thing about that is... no, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I know exactly what happens when you make a mistake in a warehouse. I've been doing this for, what, fifty years? I started back when everything was done on paper manifests, with carbon copies. You had a pink slip, a yellow slip, and the white original on a heavy clipboard. If you wrote down the wrong aisle number—say you put aisle 4 instead of aisle 14—well, somebody would go looking for the pallet, they wouldn't find it, they'd curse a little bit, and then we'd just track it down and fix the paperwork with a red pen. You initialed it, and that was that. It wasn't the end of the world. \n\nAnd it's the same today, really, even with all the technology. I mean, just last spring, we had this young kid, Tyler I think his name was, barely out of high school. He was driving the forklift and he misjudged his turning radius, clipped the edge of a rack, and brought down a whole pallet of those glass mason jars we stock for canning season. I'm telling you, it sounded like a bomb went off in there! Glass everywhere. But you know what happened? We got the push brooms, we swept it up, Kevin printed out a damage report, and Tyler went right back to work. Nobody called the National Guard, nobody lost their house over it. \n\nSo, no, I don't sit there worrying that if I tap the wrong little square on that tablet, the whole company is going to go bankrupt or something. I know logically that if I mess up a barcode scan, it just means the inventory count is off by one until the monthly audit catches it. I know the consequences of a small mistake are just that—small. That's not what's bothering me. \n\nIt's not a fear of... of some grand catastrophe. It's just the sheer frustration of not being able to do it right in the first place, knowing I'm messing up the simple things, and the way my stomach just completely drops when I have to hold that machine. But as for worrying that a little mistake is going to have some serious, life-altering consequence? No, doc, I've seen way too many mistakes in my time to think like that. You just sweep up the glass and move on."
